It’s not ruining my life or anything… I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. I still get my work done. But it does upset me more than I’d like to admit, I guess.

Yes… sometimes. I don’t want to overstate it, but once I notice it—especially in that brighter bathroom mirror—it can be hard to just let it go.

I guess I worry that if I can see it, maybe other people can too, even if they don’t say anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your perception of your face is completely accurate?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that it’s completely accurate, no. I mean… I’m here because ever since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, I keep noticing the skin around my cheeks and jaw looking uneven or kind of saggy to me. But my husband says he can’t really see what I’m talking about, so maybe I’m seeing it more harshly than other people would. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: How much distress would you say your concerns about your face are causing you?\n\nPatient: It’s not ruining my life or anything… I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. I still get my work done. But it does upset me more than I’d like to admit, I guess.\n\nI’ve been spending extra time getting ready, checking how it looks, and then I end up rushing into the courthouse some mornings. And I’ve missed the start of yoga more than once because of it, which is embarrassing. I’ve also skipped being in a few casual photos at book club or community things, just because I didn’t want to worry about how my face would look. So… mild to moderate, maybe? Does that sound like the right way to say it?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it difficult to stop thinking about the appearance of your face once you've started?\n\nPatient: Yes… sometimes. I don’t want to overstate it, but once I notice it—especially in that brighter bathroom mirror—it can be hard to just let it go.\n\nI’ll see the skin around my cheeks or jaw looking uneven or kind of saggy, and then it sort of stays in my mind. Even though my husband says he really can’t see what I’m talking about, I’ll keep wondering if it looks that way to me because it actually is noticeable. I know that may not be the most rational answer, but that’s what tends to happen.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I can control it sometimes, I think. Like, I can tell myself, “Okay, stop checking, just go get ready,” and I can move on.

I mean… I’m here because ever since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, I keep noticing the skin around my cheeks and jaw looking uneven or kind of saggy to me. But my husband says he can’t really see what I’m talking about, so maybe I’m seeing it more harshly than other people would. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: How much distress would you say your concerns about your face are causing you?\n\nPatient: It’s not ruining my life or anything… I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. I still get my work done. But it does upset me more than I’d like to admit, I guess.\n\nI’ve been spending extra time getting ready, checking how it looks, and then I end up rushing into the courthouse some mornings. And I’ve missed the start of yoga more than once because of it, which is embarrassing. I’ve also skipped being in a few casual photos at book club or community things, just because I didn’t want to worry about how my face would look. So… mild to moderate, maybe? Does that sound like the right way to say it?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it difficult to stop thinking about the appearance of your face once you've started?\n\nPatient: Yes… sometimes. I don’t want to overstate it, but once I notice it—especially in that brighter bathroom mirror—it can be hard to just let it go.\n\nI’ll see the skin around my cheeks or jaw looking uneven or kind of saggy, and then it sort of stays in my mind. Even though my husband says he really can’t see what I’m talking about, I’ll keep wondering if it looks that way to me because it actually is noticeable. I know that may not be the most rational answer, but that’s what tends to happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can control how much you think about the appearance of your face?\n\nPatient: I can control it sometimes, I think. Like, I can tell myself, “Okay, stop checking, just go get ready,” and I can move on.\n\nBut if I catch it in that brighter bathroom mirror—the skin around my cheeks or my jaw looking uneven or saggy—then it’s harder. It sort of hooks my attention, even though my husband says he doesn’t really see what I’m talking about. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but no, I don’t always feel fully in control of it. Does that answer what you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really avoiding people altogether, no. I mean, I still go to work and see people, and I get done what I need to get done.

But… I guess there have been some ways it affects things. I’ve spent longer getting ready than I meant to, and then I’m rushing into the courthouse in the morning, or I’ve missed the beginning of yoga more than once. And I have skipped being in a few casual photos, like at book club or community events, because I didn’t want to be worrying about how my face was going to look.

So not avoiding social interactions exactly… but I suppose I do avoid certain situations around them. Does that make sense?

I’m still getting my work done and everything. But I have been spending longer getting ready, and then I’ve had to rush into the courthouse a few mornings, which isn’t like me. And I’ve missed the start of yoga more than once because I was still fussing with how I looked.\n\nSocially, it’s not huge, but… I have skipped being in a few casual photos at book club or community events. I just didn’t want to have to worry afterward about how my face looked. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about the uneven or saggy appearance of your face that you wish you could change?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, mostly I wish the skin around my cheeks and along my jaw looked smoother or firmer. I mean, I’m here because since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, I keep noticing the skin around my cheeks and jaw—like it looks uneven or kind of saggy to me. My husband says he can’t really see what I’m talking about, so I know it may be me focusing too much, but that’s the thing I keep coming back to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people can see the unevenness or sagging of the skin around your cheeks and jaw when they look at you?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure. I mean, my husband says he really can’t see what I’m talking about, so maybe it’s not obvious to other people. I don’t want to make it sound like it’s constant, because it isn’t. I mean, I’m here because since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, I keep noticing the skin around my cheeks and jaw—like it looks uneven or kind of saggy to me. My husband says he can’t really see what I’m talking about, so I know it may be me focusing too much, but that’s the thing I keep coming back to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people can see the unevenness or sagging of the skin around your cheeks and jaw when they look at you?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure. No, I don’t think I’ve avoided anything really important, like a wedding or work event or anything like that. I still get my work done.

It’s more small things… like I’ve taken too long getting ready and then had to rush into the courthouse a few mornings, which feels embarrassing. And I’ve missed the start of yoga more than once because I was fussing with how I looked.

I’m still getting my work done and everything. But I have been spending longer getting ready, and then I’ve had to rush into the courthouse a few mornings, which isn’t like me. And I’ve missed the start of yoga more than once because I was still fussing with how I looked.\n\nSocially, it’s not huge, but… I have skipped being in a few casual photos at book club or community events. In that brighter bathroom mirror it just looks uneven to me, kind of saggy, even though my husband keeps saying he doesn’t really see it. I know it might not sound like much, but that’s the part I keep wanting to somehow fix.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about your face focused on this one issue with the skin around your cheeks and jaw?\n\nPatient: Yes, mostly that one area. I mean, I’m here because since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, I keep noticing the skin around my cheeks and jaw—like it looks uneven or kind of saggy to me. My husband says he can’t really see what I’m talking about, so I know it may be me focusing too much, but that’s the thing I keep coming back to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people can see the unevenness or sagging of the skin around your cheeks and jaw when they look at you?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure. I mean, my husband says he really can’t see what I’m talking about, so maybe it’s not obvious to other people. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi… um, I guess it might sound a little silly, but I’ve been kind of bothered by my face lately. We put in a brighter mirror in the bathroom, and since then I keep noticing the skin around my cheeks and jaw looks uneven, or maybe a bit saggy to me. My husband says he doesn’t really see what I’m talking about, so I don’t know if I’m overreacting, but it’s been on my mind enough that I thought I should come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about your face a lot throughout the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I try not to make it a big deal, but since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, I do keep noticing it. Mostly the skin around my cheeks and my jaw—like it looks uneven or a little saggy to me. My husband says he really can’t see what I’m talking about, so maybe I’m just being too focused on it, but… it does pop into my mind a lot.\n\nDoctor: Has thinking about your face been affecting your daily activities or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: A little, I guess… I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. I’m still getting my work done and everything. But I have been spending longer getting ready, and then I’ve had to rush into the courthouse a few mornings, which isn’t like me. And I’ve missed the start of yoga more than once because I was still fussing with how I looked.\n\nSocially, it’s not huge, but… I have skipped being in a few casual photos at book club or community events. I just didn’t want to have to worry afterward about how my face looked. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about the uneven or saggy appearance of your face that you wish you could change?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, mostly I wish the skin around my cheeks and along my jaw looked smoother or firmer. In that brighter bathroom mirror it just looks uneven to me, kind of saggy, even though my husband keeps saying he doesn’t really see it. I know it might not sound like much, but that’s the part I keep wanting to somehow fix.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about your face focused on this one issue with the skin around your cheeks and jaw?\n\nPatient: Yes, mostly that one area. I mean, I’m here because since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, I keep noticing the skin around my cheeks and jaw—like it looks uneven or kind of saggy to me. My husband says he can’t really see what I’m talking about, so I know it may be me focusing too much, but that’s the thing I keep coming back to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people can see the unevenness or sagging of the skin around your cheeks and jaw when they look at you?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure. I mean, my husband says he really can’t see what I’m talking about, so maybe it’s not obvious to other people. But since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, I notice it a lot—the skin around my cheeks and jaw just looks uneven or a little saggy to me. I guess I worry that if I can see it, maybe other people can too, even if they don’t say anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your perception of your face is completely accurate?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that it’s completely accurate, no. I mean… I’m here because ever since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, I keep noticing the skin around my cheeks and jaw looking uneven or kind of saggy to me. But my husband says he can’t really see what I’m talking about, so maybe I’m seeing it more harshly than other people would. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: How much distress would you say your concerns about your face are causing you?\n\nPatient: It’s not ruining my life or anything… I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. I still get my work done. But it does upset me more than I’d like to admit, I guess.\n\nI’ve been spending extra time getting ready, checking how it looks, and then I end up rushing into the courthouse some mornings. And I’ve missed the start of yoga more than once because of it, which is embarrassing. I’ve also skipped being in a few casual photos at book club or community things, just because I didn’t want to worry about how my face would look. So… mild to moderate, maybe? Does that sound like the right way to say it?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it difficult to stop thinking about the appearance of your face once you've started?\n\nPatient: Yes… sometimes. I don’t want to overstate it, but once I notice it—especially in that brighter bathroom mirror—it can be hard to just let it go.\n\nI’ll see the skin around my cheeks or jaw looking uneven or kind of saggy, and then it sort of stays in my mind. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that thoughts about your face's appearance force their way into your mind even when you're trying to focus on other things?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, a little. I don’t want to make it sound like it’s constant, because it isn’t. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts about the appearance of your face are interfering with your ability to concentrate on other things?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t want to overstate it. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi… um, I guess it might sound a little silly, but I’ve been kind of bothered by my face lately. We put in a brighter mirror in the bathroom, and since then I keep noticing the skin around my cheeks and jaw looks uneven, or maybe a bit saggy to me. My husband says he doesn’t really see what I’m talking about, so I don’t know if I’m overreacting, but it’s been on my mind enough that I thought I should come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about your face a lot throughout the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I try not to make it a big deal, but since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, I do keep noticing it. Mostly the skin around my cheeks and my jaw—like it looks uneven or a little saggy to me. My husband says he really can’t see what I’m talking about, so maybe I’m just being too focused on it, but… it does pop into my mind a lot.\n\nDoctor: Has thinking about your face been affecting your daily activities or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: A little, I guess… I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. I’m still getting my work done and everything. But I have been spending longer getting ready, and then I’ve had to rush into the courthouse a few mornings, which isn’t like me. And I’ve missed the start of yoga more than once because I was still fussing with how I looked.\n\nSocially, it’s not huge, but… I have skipped being in a few casual photos at book club or community events. I just didn’t want to have to worry afterward about how my face looked. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about the uneven or saggy appearance of your face that you wish you could change?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, mostly I wish the skin around my cheeks and along my jaw looked smoother or firmer. In that brighter bathroom mirror it just looks uneven to me, kind of saggy, even though my husband keeps saying he doesn’t really see it. I know it might not sound like much, but that’s the part I keep wanting to somehow fix.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about your face focused on this one issue with the skin around your cheeks and jaw?\n\nPatient: Yes, mostly that one area. I mean, I’m here because since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, I keep noticing the skin around my cheeks and jaw—like it looks uneven or kind of saggy to me. My husband says he can’t really see what I’m talking about, so I know it may be me focusing too much, but that’s the thing I keep coming back to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people can see the unevenness or sagging of the skin around your cheeks and jaw when they look at you?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure. I mean, my husband says he really can’t see what I’m talking about, so maybe it’s not obvious to other people. But since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, I notice it a lot—the skin around my cheeks and jaw just looks uneven or a little saggy to me. I guess I worry that if I can see it, maybe other people can too, even if they don’t say anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your perception of your face is completely accurate?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that it’s completely accurate, no. I mean… I’m here because ever since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, I keep noticing the skin around my cheeks and jaw looking uneven or kind of saggy to me. But my husband says he can’t really see what I’m talking about, so maybe I’m seeing it more harshly than other people would. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: How much distress would you say your concerns about your face are causing you?\n\nPatient: It’s not ruining my life or anything… I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. I still get my work done. But it does upset me more than I’d like to admit, I guess.\n\nI’ve been spending extra time getting ready, checking how it looks, and then I end up rushing into the courthouse some mornings. And I’ve missed the start of yoga more than once because of it, which is embarrassing. I’ve also skipped being in a few casual photos at book club or community things, just because I didn’t want to worry about how my face would look. So… mild to moderate, maybe? Does that sound like the right way to say it?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it difficult to stop thinking about the appearance of your face once you've started?\n\nPatient: Yes… sometimes. I don’t want to overstate it, but once I notice it—especially in that brighter bathroom mirror—it can be hard to just let it go.\n\nI’ll see the skin around my cheeks or jaw looking uneven or kind of saggy, and then it sort of stays in my mind. Even though my husband says he really can’t see what I’m talking about, I’ll keep wondering if it looks that way to me because it actually is noticeable. I know that may not be the most rational answer, but that’s what tends to happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can control how much you think about the appearance of your face?\n\nPatient: I can control it sometimes, I think. Like, I can tell myself, “Okay, stop checking, just go get ready,” and I can move on.\n\nBut if I catch it in that brighter bathroom mirror—the skin around my cheeks or my jaw looking uneven or saggy—then it’s harder. It sort of hooks my attention, even though my husband says he doesn’t really see what I’m talking about. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but no, I don’t always feel fully in control of it. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that thoughts about your face's appearance force their way into your mind even when you're trying to focus on other things?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, a little. I don’t want to make it sound like it’s constant, because it isn’t. But ever since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, I’ll notice the skin around my cheeks and jaw looking uneven or kind of saggy to me, and then it can pop back into my mind later even when I’m trying to focus on something else.\n\nMy husband says he can’t really see what I’m talking about, so I know maybe I’m making too much of it… but it does kind of force its way in sometimes. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding social interactions because of concerns about how your face looks?\n\nPatient: Not really avoiding people altogether, no. I mean, I still go to work and see people, and I get done what I need to get done.\n\nBut… I guess there have been some ways it affects things. I’ve spent longer getting ready than I meant to, and then I’m rushing into the courthouse in the morning, or I’ve missed the beginning of yoga more than once. And I have skipped being in a few casual photos, like at book club or community events, because I didn’t want to be worrying about how my face was going to look.\n\nSo not avoiding social interactions exactly… but I suppose I do avoid certain situations around them. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts about the appearance of your face are interfering with your ability to concentrate on other things?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t want to overstate it. It’s mostly since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror. I’ll notice the skin around my cheeks and jaw looking uneven or kind of saggy, at least to me, and then I keep thinking about it afterward when I’m supposed to be focusing on something else.\n\nMy husband says he can’t really see what I’m talking about, so I know it may not be as noticeable as it feels to me. But yes, it can distract me. Is that the kind of interference you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have your concerns about your face's appearance caused you to avoid any important life events or activities?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve avoided anything really important, like a wedding or work event or anything like that. I still get my work done.\n\nIt’s more small things… like I’ve taken too long getting ready and then had to rush into the courthouse a few mornings, which feels embarrassing. And I’ve missed the start of yoga more than once because I was fussing with how I looked.\n\nI’ve also skipped being in a few casual photos at book club or community events, just because I didn’t want to spend the rest of the day worrying about how my face would look in them. But I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding major life events… if that’s the distinction you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is there anything else about your appearance, besides the skin around your cheeks and jaw, that you've been concerned about?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, it’s mostly just that same area I’ve mentioned — my cheeks and along my jaw. Since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, I keep noticing it looking uneven or a little saggy to me.\n\nMy husband says he can’t really see what I mean, so maybe I’m making too much of it. But that’s the area I keep focusing on.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to stop worrying about your face if you were able to achieve the smoother, firmer look you desire?\n\nPatient: I think… probably? I mean, if the skin around my cheeks and jaw looked smoother or firmer, I’d hope I wouldn’t keep noticing it so much.\n\nIt’s just that ever since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, I keep seeing it as uneven or kind of saggy, even though my husband says he doesn’t really see what I’m talking about. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi… um, I guess it might sound a little silly, but I’ve been kind of bothered by my face lately. My husband says he doesn’t really see what I’m talking about, so I don’t know if I’m overreacting, but it’s been on my mind enough that I thought I should come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about your face a lot throughout the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I try not to make it a big deal, but since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, I do keep noticing it. Mostly the skin around my cheeks and my jaw—like it looks uneven or a little saggy to me. My husband says he really can’t see what I’m talking about, so maybe I’m just being too focused on it, but… it does pop into my mind a lot.\n\nDoctor: Has thinking about your face been affecting your daily activities or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: A little, I guess… I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. I’m still getting my work done and everything. But I have been spending longer getting ready, and then I’ve had to rush into the courthouse a few mornings, which isn’t like me. And I’ve missed the start of yoga more than once because I was still fussing with how I looked.\n\nSocially, it’s not huge, but… I have skipped being in a few casual photos at book club or community events. I just didn’t want to have to worry afterward about how my face looked. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about the uneven or saggy appearance of your face that you wish you could change?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, mostly I wish the skin around my cheeks and along my jaw looked smoother or firmer. In that brighter bathroom mirror it just looks uneven to me, kind of saggy, even though my husband keeps saying he doesn’t really see it. I know it might not sound like much, but that’s the part I keep wanting to somehow fix.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about your face focused on this one issue with the skin around your cheeks and jaw?\n\nPatient: Yes, mostly that one area. I mean, I’m here because since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, I keep noticing the skin around my cheeks and jaw—like it looks uneven or kind of saggy to me. My husband says he can’t really see what I’m talking about, so I know it may be me focusing too much, but that’s the thing I keep coming back to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people can see the unevenness or sagging of the skin around your cheeks and jaw when they look at you?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure. I mean, my husband says he really can’t see what I’m talking about, so maybe it’s not obvious to other people. But since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, I notice it a lot—the skin around my cheeks and jaw just looks uneven or a little saggy to me. I guess I worry that if I can see it, maybe other people can too, even if they don’t say anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your perception of your face is completely accurate?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that it’s completely accurate, no. I mean… I’m here because ever since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, I keep noticing the skin around my cheeks and jaw looking uneven or kind of saggy to me. But my husband says he can’t really see what I’m talking about, so maybe I’m seeing it more harshly than other people would. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: How much distress would you say your concerns about your face are causing you?\n\nPatient: It’s not ruining my life or anything… I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. I still get my work done. But it does upset me more than I’d like to admit, I guess.\n\nI’ve been spending extra time getting ready, checking how it looks, and then I end up rushing into the courthouse some mornings. And I’ve missed the start of yoga more than once because of it, which is embarrassing. I’ve also skipped being in a few casual photos at book club or community things, just because I didn’t want to worry about how my face would look. So… mild to moderate, maybe? Does that sound like the right way to say it?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it difficult to stop thinking about the appearance of your face once you've started?\n\nPatient: Yes… sometimes. I don’t want to overstate it, but once I notice it—especially in that brighter bathroom mirror—it can be hard to just let it go.\n\nI’ll see the skin around my cheeks or jaw looking uneven or kind of saggy, and then it sort of stays in my mind. Even though my husband says he really can’t see what I’m talking about, I’ll keep wondering if it looks that way to me because it actually is noticeable. I know that may not be the most rational answer, but that’s what tends to happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can control how much you think about the appearance of your face?\n\nPatient: I can control it sometimes, I think. Like, I can tell myself, “Okay, stop checking, just go get ready,” and I can move on.\n\nBut if I catch it in that brighter bathroom mirror—the skin around my cheeks or my jaw looking uneven or saggy—then it’s harder. It sort of hooks my attention, even though my husband says he doesn’t really see what I’m talking about. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but no, I don’t always feel fully in control of it. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that thoughts about your face's appearance force their way into your mind even when you're trying to focus on other things?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, a little. I don’t want to make it sound like it’s constant, because it isn’t. But ever since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, I’ll notice the skin around my cheeks and jaw looking uneven or kind of saggy to me, and then it can pop back into my mind later even when I’m trying to focus on something else.\n\nMy husband says he can’t really see what I’m talking about, so I know maybe I’m making too much of it… but it does kind of force its way in sometimes. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding social interactions because of concerns about how your face looks?\n\nPatient: Not really avoiding people altogether, no. I mean, I still go to work and see people, and I get done what I need to get done.\n\nBut… I guess there have been some ways it affects things. I’ve spent longer getting ready than I meant to, and then I’m rushing into the courthouse in the morning, or I’ve missed the beginning of yoga more than once. And I have skipped being in a few casual photos, like at book club or community events, because I didn’t want to be worrying about how my face was going to look.\n\nSo not avoiding social interactions exactly… but I suppose I do avoid certain situations around them. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts about the appearance of your face are interfering with your ability to concentrate on other things?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t want to overstate it. It’s mostly since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror. I’ll notice the skin around my cheeks and jaw looking uneven or kind of saggy, at least to me, and then I keep thinking about it afterward when I’m supposed to be focusing on something else.\n\nMy husband says he can’t really see what I’m talking about, so I know it may not be as noticeable as it feels to me. But yes, it can distract me. Is that the kind of interference you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have your concerns about your face's appearance caused you to avoid any important life events or activities?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve avoided anything really important, like a wedding or work event or anything like that. I still get my work done.\n\nIt’s more small things… like I’ve taken too long getting ready and then had to rush into the courthouse a few mornings, which feels embarrassing. And I’ve missed the start of yoga more than once because I was fussing with how I looked.\n\nI’ve also skipped being in a few casual photos at book club or community events, just because I didn’t want to spend the rest of the day worrying about how my face would look in them. But I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding major life events… if that’s the distinction you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is there anything else about your appearance, besides the skin around your cheeks and jaw, that you've been concerned about?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, it’s mostly just that same area I’ve mentioned — my cheeks and along my jaw. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that thoughts about your face's appearance force their way into your mind even when you're trying to focus on other things?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, a little. But ever since we put in that brighter bathroom mirror, I’ll notice the skin around my cheeks and jaw looking uneven or kind of saggy to me, and then it can pop back into my mind later even when I’m trying to focus on something else.\n\nMy husband says he can’t really see what I’m talking about, so I know maybe I’m making too much of it… but it does kind of force its way in sometimes. I’ll notice the skin around my cheeks and jaw looking uneven or kind of saggy, at least to me, and then I keep thinking about it afterward when I’m supposed to be focusing on something else.\n\nMy husband says he can’t really see what I’m talking about, so I know it may not be as noticeable as it feels to me. But yes, it can distract me. Is that the kind of interference you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have your concerns about your face's appearance caused you to avoid any important life events or activities?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve avoided anything really important, like a wedding or work event or anything like that.
